Lesson-specific corrections include factual, algorithmic, caption, and diagram-layout fixes. The factual/algorithm total includes documented caption errors. Shared wrappers and display support are counted separately and do not classify every lesson as erroneous. Counts overlap across categories.

What changed across the site
  • Ignore stale speech events after cancellation or watchdog recovery so chunks are not skipped and progress is not counted twice.
  • Correct the slow-motion description to match its actual 40% speed.
  • Pin corrected narration source hashes and reject stale recorded explanations until the replacement matches.
  • Correct the home page to distinguish streamed recordings from on-device browser speech.
  • Check visualization invariants on the terminal tick, not only earlier ticks.
  • Live reduced-motion preference changes; stored choices still win; listener cleanup.
  • Rounded pixel transforms and density changes keep canvases sharp; no advance before sizing and visibility; finished intervals stop.
  • Enlarge/fit controls wrap all registered canvases and shared static figures; keyboard scroll and unique accessible control relations.
  • Native font and transformed text-bound verification remains an optional tooling dependency.
  • Later canvas labels shortened or deliberately wrapped without global font reduction; captions and summaries separated.
  • RRT and AdaBoost footer spacing; Perceptron separator clipped to the disk; RS search row separated from its caption.
  • Binomial and BDD footer room; CG plot labels separated; Barnes-Hut complete unit-square tree bounds inset and plotting strokes clipped.
  • AVL depth fitted into its panel; Holt-Winters caption kept under the plot; Brent and Shunting Yard labels separated.
  • Vickrey negative overbid utility preserved below a zero baseline; actual callback regression covers gains and losses.
  • Template Matching captions below the heatmaps; Importance Sampling panel legends separated; LL(1) table fits; Vertex Cover stats clear the graph.
  • Long complexity chips and metric cards wrap within narrow page widths.

Verification

205 current Python solutions verified, 205 pages rendered, 4,100 animation runs checked, and 615 visual samples inspected. The static-diagram browser check covered 3,041 labels with no clipping or overlap warnings.

Recordings: All 25 lessons published and live-verified. 25 lessons, 50 tracks. Conservative synthesis ceiling: $16.54.

Scope and limits

Final build and checks: passed.

  • This review and executable evidence do not constitute a formal proof of every statement or all inputs.
  • The actual-callback harness stubs browser effects. Separate native rasterization, browser review, and text-bound checks cover visual presentation.
  • Bounds tests detect canvas-edge clipping and configured SVG text-rectangle intersections. Manual review covers sampled shape relationships; every possible animation frame and input is not exhaustively rendered.
  • Probabilistic demonstrations and numerical tolerances were checked on deterministic tested seeds; their success is not a universal guarantee.

Source data: complete JSON audit record.

008Union-find / Union by rank with path compressionTechnical correction

A forest of parent pointers where rank decides who hangs under whom and every lookup flattens the path it walked, until each question costs almost nothing.

Open lesson

  • Replace the fixed three-hop interpretation with amortized O(alpha(n)); an individual find can take more hops. Qualify the spoken Fredman-Saks lower bound as asymptotic and model-dependent.
Text and code corrections (1)

src/content/unionfind-rank-compression.narration.js

Before
'The strength is that the cost is effectively constant, and provably so. Amortized inverse Ackermann per operation: three hops at fifteen hundred elements, at most four at the scale of the universe, and Fredman and Saks showed nothing can do better. The weakness is what the structure refuses to know. It answers together or not yet, and nothing else. It cannot split, cannot undo, and cannot tell you the route between two connected elements, only that one exists. The moment edges can be deleted you need entirely different machinery, link cut trees or Euler tour trees, at logarithmic cost per operation. And the guarantee is amortized, not per operation: a single early find can still be slow; it is the average over the stream that is nearly free.',
After
'The strength is that the cost is effectively constant, and provably so. Amortized big O of inverse Ackermann per operation. The inverse Ackermann function is tiny at any practical size, but its value is not an exact hop count. Fredman and Saks proved a matching asymptotic lower bound in their model. The weakness is what the structure refuses to know. It answers together or not yet, and nothing else. It cannot split, cannot undo, and cannot tell you the route between two connected elements, only that one exists. The moment edges can be deleted you need entirely different machinery, link cut trees or Euler tour trees, at logarithmic cost per operation. And the guarantee is amortized, not per operation: a single early find can still be slow; it is the average over the stream that is nearly free.',
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/unionfind-rank-compression.jsx

Before
What each heuristic is for. Quick-find pays the full array on every effective union, a flat wall at n. Naive linking is cheap until merge order turns the forest into a chain, where one find walks all 1,500 parents. Rank caps the height at log n, and compression flattens every path a query walks, so the pairing's cost sits on the floor: amortized inverse Ackermann, at most 3 hops at this size.
After
What each heuristic is for. Quick-find pays the full array on every effective union, a flat wall at n. Naive linking is cheap until merge order turns the forest into a chain, where one find walks all 1,500 parents. Rank caps the height at log n, and compression flattens every path a query walks, so the pairing's cost sits on the floor: amortized O(α(n)). This bounds a sequence of operations; an individual find can take more hops.

After

010Quicksort / Median-of-three pivotTechnical correction

Partition around a pivot and recurse; three sampled keys per split are the cheap insurance that keeps a great average case from meeting its quadratic ghost.

Open lesson

  • Process the smaller partition first and retain only nontrivial ranges, supporting the stated logarithmic stack bound.
  • State that this median-of-three routine uses two or three comparisons, matching its actual branches and visualization counter.
  • Count actual two-or-three median sampling comparisons.

Animation correction: Count actual two-or-three median sampling comparisons.

Metadata corrections (1)

time

Before

After

Text and code corrections (2)

src/data/puzzles.js

Before
O(n log n) expected
After
O(n log n) average; O(n²) worst

src/content/quicksort-median-of-three.jsx

Before
An
      explicit stack works as well as recursion and survives bad splits.
After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/quicksort-median-of-three.jsx

Before
The pivot does not decide whether quicksort is correct; it decides the shape of the recursion. Median-ish pivots produce the left shape: log n levels, n work per level. A near-minimum pivot produces the right shape: each partition retires one element, n levels deep, n²/2 total. Median-of-three spends three comparisons per partition to buy the left shape on the inputs production actually serves: sorted, reversed, and nearly ordered data.
After
The pivot does not decide whether quicksort is correct; it decides the shape of the recursion. Median-ish pivots produce the left shape: log n levels, n work per level. A near-minimum pivot produces the right shape: each partition retires one element, n levels deep, n²/2 total. Median-of-three spends two or three comparisons per partition to buy the left shape on the inputs production actually serves: sorted, reversed, and nearly ordered data.

After
Median-of-three spends two or three comparisons per partition

After

012HyperLogLog / Leading-zero registersTechnical correction

A kilobyte of registers counts a million-item stream: the longest run of leading zeros ever seen is a witness statement about how many distinct hashes went by.

Open lesson

  • Define rank as leading zeros plus one; distinguish standard error from an absolute bound and modeled packed bytes from actual bytearray storage.
Metadata corrections (1)

space

Before
768 B at ±3.3%
After
1,024 register bytes here; about 3.3% relative SE
Text and code corrections (1)

src/data/puzzles.js

Before
768 B at ±3.3%
After
1,024 register bytes here; about 3.3% relative SE
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/hyperloglog-leading-zeros.jsx

Before
Rarity as a census. Each hash is a run of fair coin flips; a run of r leading zeros appears about once per 2^r distinct hashes, so the longest run a register has ever seen testifies to how many passed through it. Repeats are powerless: the same item produces the same bits, and a maximum cannot be raised by what it already saw. One witness is noisy; 1,024 of them, combined by a harmonic mean that refuses to be impressed by a single lucky run, land within a few percent.
After
Rarity as a census. Each hash is a run of fair coin flips; a run of at least r leading zeros appears about once per 2^r distinct hashes. A register stores the maximum run length plus one, with zero reserved for an untouched register. Repeats are powerless: the same item produces the same bits, and a maximum cannot be raised by what it already saw. One witness is noisy; 1,024 of them, combined by a harmonic mean that refuses to be impressed by a single lucky run, give about 3.3% relative standard error, not a guaranteed error bound.
Review and verification

Model: HLL register ranks, remaining hash bits and low-range correction.

Checks: Source and actual callback review.

Python: RETEST_PASS. Page render: passed. Animation: Passed actual init, tick, draw, and terminal checks.

Static figure and caption reviewed; three raster samples inspected. PASS: catalog-wide DOM rectangle check.

Enlarge/fit controls, device-pixel-ratio refitting, and visibility/motion lifecycle support; these shared upgrades do not imply a lesson-specific error.

Audio: No recorded regeneration required by this audit

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/pagerank-damped-walk.jsx

Before
What the damping dial buys. Left: a spider trap under the pure walk: probability flows in, circulates, and never leaves, so the loop's share of the ranking grows without bound (84.7% measured). Right: the same trap under the damped walk: every page continuously exhales 15% of its mass to everywhere, so the trap leaks exactly as fast as any neighborhood its size deserves, and the chain acquires a unique stationary answer reachable at a known geometric rate.

Before
D² sampling in one frame. One seed is placed; every other point's brightness is its squared distance to the nearest seed so far, which is exactly its probability of becoming the next seed. 022Binary search / Halving invariantTechnical correction

Keep one true sentence, "the answer lies in [lo, hi)", and probe the midpoint so either answer halves it: twenty probes on a million keys, guaranteed, on any input.

Open lesson

  • The insertion index remains in the closed interval [lo, hi], including hi; only the unexamined array elements use [lo, hi).
Text and code corrections (3)

src/content/binary-search-halving.jsx

Before
the
      answer&apos;s index lies in [lo, hi).
After
the insertion index lies in [lo, hi], including hi; the unexamined elements lie in [lo, hi).

src/content/binary-search-halving.narration.js

Before
a half open interval, lo inclusive, hi exclusive, together with one claim that must never stop being true: the answer’s position lies inside the bracket.
After
a half open interval of unexamined elements, lo inclusive and hi exclusive. The insertion position can also equal hi, so its invariant is the closed interval from lo through hi.

src/content/binary-search-halving.narration.js

Before
the answer lies in the half open interval from lo to hi.
After
the insertion position lies from lo through hi, including both endpoints.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/binary-search-halving.jsx

Before
The invariant is the algorithm. The claim [lo, hi) contains the answer is restored after every probe: a[mid] < target discards mid itself (lo = mid + 1), anything else keeps mid as a candidate (hi = mid). Both famous failures are clause violations: dropping the +1 stops the bracket from shrinking, and on two elements the loop spins forever; computing the midpoint as (lo+hi)/2 in fixed-width arithmetic overflowed Java's library for nine years.
After
The invariant is the algorithm. The insertion-index interval [lo, hi] contains the answer after every probe: a[mid] < target discards mid itself (lo = mid + 1), anything else keeps mid as a candidate (hi = mid). Both famous failures are clause violations: dropping the +1 stops the bracket from shrinking, and on two elements the loop spins forever; computing the midpoint as (lo+hi)/2 in fixed-width arithmetic overflowed Java's library for nine years.

After

src/content/kruskal-union-find.jsx

Before
The cut property, the one-sentence proof behind the greedy. Slice the vertices into two sides any way you like: the lightest edge crossing the slice belongs to every minimum spanning tree, because a tree omitting it must cross the slice somewhere heavier, and swapping the two strictly improves it. Kruskal's scan order makes every accepted edge the lightest crossing of the cut between the components it joins, so every acceptance is this argument, applied.
After
The cut property, the one-sentence proof behind the greedy. Slice the vertices into two sides any way you like: a uniquely lightest edge crossing the slice belongs to every minimum spanning tree, because a tree omitting it must cross the slice somewhere heavier, and swapping the two strictly improves it. With tied lightest edges, any one can be included in some minimum spanning tree. Kruskal's scan order makes every accepted edge a lightest crossing of the cut between the components it joins, so each acceptance preserves a minimum spanning tree extension.

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/simplex-dantzig-pivots.jsx

Before
The trap, at n = 3. Klee and Minty squash a cube so that from every vertex, the steepest-starting edge belongs to the long way around: Dantzig's climber visits all 2³ corners in 7 pivots, each step locally irreproachable, while a two-pivot route to the top sat there the whole time. After
The trap, at n = 3. Klee and Minty squash a cube so that from every vertex, the edge with the largest gain per unit of its entering variable belongs to the long way around: Dantzig's climber visits all 2³ corners in 7 pivots, each step locally irreproachable, while the illustrated three-pivot route to the top sat there the whole time. The construction scales: 2ⁿ − 1 pivots, measured on this page up to 4,095 at n = 12, while the random rule takes 39 pivots on the measured n = 12 instance. That changes the pivot rule, not the coefficients.

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/viterbi-max-product.jsx

Before
One cell of the trellis, and the semiring that defines the question. The best path into state s at time t is the max over predecessors of their best paths times the transition, times the emission; backpointers remember the argmax and the backtrace reads the story out. Change exactly one operation, max to sum, and the identical lattice answers a different question: the total weight of all stories, which is the forward algorithm, posterior decoding, and the door to training.
After
One cell of the trellis, and the semiring that defines the question. The best path into state s at time t is the max over predecessors of their best paths times the transition, times the emission; backpointers remember the argmax and the backtrace reads the story out. Change exactly one operation, max to sum, and the identical lattice answers a different question: the total weight of all stories, which is the forward algorithm; add a backward pass for posterior decoding, and the door to training.
Review and verification

Model: Viterbi max-product backpointers; marginals require forward-backward.

Before
The cursor, the window, and the back-reference. Everything left of the cursor is already-written output; the window is its recent tail. A match becomes a (distance, length) token: an instruction to the decoder to copy from its own past. The flag bit (LZSS) says which kind of token follows. Repetition beyond the window is invisible on purpose: that blindness is the price of fixed memory, and the measured dial shows what each kilobyte of reach buys.
After
The cursor, the window, and the back-reference. Everything left of the cursor is already-written output; the window is its recent tail. A match becomes a (distance, length) token: an instruction to the decoder to copy from its own past. The flag bit (LZSS) says which kind of token follows. Repetition beyond the window is invisible on purpose: that bounds the history a streaming decoder needs, and the measured dial shows what each kilobyte of reach buys. The teaching encoder retains O(n) history and token storage.

042MinHash / Bottom-k signaturesTechnical correction

Give every element a lottery ticket: the room’s lowest belongs to both sets with probability exactly J, and keeping the k lowest runs k lotteries from one drawing.

Open lesson

  • Keep only k distinct hash values while consuming the stream, rather than storing and sorting the complete input.
  • Bottom-k retains distinct hashes from one shared ordering and samples without replacement. Conventional banding uses a separate signature of independent MinHash functions.
Metadata corrections (1)

time

Before
O(|S|) sketch, O(k) compare
After
O(|S| log k) sketch, O(k log k) compare
Text and code corrections (11)

src/data/puzzles.js

Before
O(|S|) sketch, O(k) compare
After
O(|S| log k) sketch, O(k log k) compare

solutions/minhash_bottom_k.py

Before
import hashlib
After
import hashlib
import heapq

solutions/minhash_bottom_k.py

Before
    hs = []
    for it in items:
        if counter is not None:
            counter["hashes"] = counter.get("hashes", 0) + 1
        hs.append(h64(it, salt))
    hs.sort()
    out = []
    for v in hs:  # dedupe (sets may repeat nothing, but hashes could tie)
        if not out or v != out[-1]:
            out.append(v)
        if len(out) == k:
            break
    return out
After
    if k <= 0:
        raise ValueError("k must be positive")
    heap, kept = [], set()
    for it in items:
        if counter is not None:
            counter["hashes"] = counter.get("hashes", 0) + 1
        value = h64(it, salt)
        if value in kept:
            continue
        if len(heap) < k:
            heapq.heappush(heap, -value)
            kept.add(value)
        elif value < -heap[0]:
            removed = -heapq.heapreplace(heap, -value)
            kept.remove(removed)
            kept.add(value)
    return sorted(kept)

src/content/minhash-bottom-k.jsx

Before
The collision theorem. Hash every element of both sets with one random function; the minimum of the union is a uniformly random element of the union, so the two sets report the same minimum exactly when that element is shared: P[collision] = J. Measured here at 0.3340 against a true 1/3 over 2,000 independent hash functions. Bottom-k runs k such lotteries from one drawing; banding turns agreement into an index.
After
The collision theorem. It samples without replacement; conventional banding instead groups signatures from independent hash functions.

043Hopcroft-Karp / Layered augmenting phasesTechnical correction

Layer the whole graph in one BFS wave, flip a batch of disjoint shortest augmenting paths at once: about √V waves close the fair, and the failed search is the certificate.

Animation correction: Enforce shortest layers and terminate DFS correctly.

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/hopcroft-karp-layered-phases.jsx

Before
One phase. BFS from every free left vertex layers the graph by alternating distance: forward on unmatched edges, backward on matched ones: stopping at the first layer containing a free right vertex. A DFS then harvests vertex-disjoint shortest augmenting paths and flips the whole batch. The shortest length strictly grows each phase, so ~2√V phases suffice: the measured graph needed 4. The final failed BFS is not waste: its reachable set IS König's minimum vertex cover, the certificate.
After
One phase. BFS from every free left vertex layers the graph by alternating distance: forward on unmatched edges, backward on matched ones: stopping at the first layer containing a free right vertex. A DFS then harvests vertex-disjoint shortest augmenting paths and flips the whole batch. The shortest length strictly grows each phase, so ~2√V phases suffice: the measured graph needed 5. The final failed BFS is not waste: the unreached left vertices together with the reached right vertices form König's minimum vertex cover, the certificate.

048Earliest deadline first / Dynamic deadline priorityTechnical correction

Rank every job by whose clock runs out next, preempt freely: utilization up to 100% is schedulable, which no fixed priority can promise.

Open lesson

  • Distinguish the finite-task rate-monotonic sufficient bound from its asymptotic limit and state the scheduling assumptions.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/earliest-deadline-first.jsx

Before
The gap, drawn. Below ln 2 ≈ 69.3%, both policies are perfect (measured). Above it, fixed priorities enter the conditional zone: the measured survival curve falls to 48% by full load, while EDF holds 100% all the way to U = 1: Liu and Layland's two theorems as one picture. Past U = 1 nobody is safe, and the two policies fail in opposite styles: EDF sprays, RM shields its favorites.
After
The gap, drawn. Below ln 2 ≈ 69.3%, both policies are perfect (measured). Rate-monotonic's finite-n sufficient bound is higher, n(2^(1/n) - 1); above that, schedulability depends on the set. The measured survival curve falls to 48% by full load, while EDF holds 100% all the way to U = 1. Past U = 1 nobody is safe, and these two policies fail in opposite styles on the measured example: EDF sprays, RM shields its favorites.
Review and verification

Model: EDF and rate-monotonic scheduling; expired jobs model discarded misses.

059Boyer-Moore majority vote / Pairwise cancellationTechnical correction

Every decrement pairs the candidate with a stranger and both walk out: a faction holding more than half the hall cannot run out of partners first.

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/boyer-moore-majority-vote.jsx

Before
Pairwise cancellation. Every decrement pairs one copy of the candidate with one different element and discards both: and deleting two different values never changes who holds a majority. A faction with m > n/2 members cannot be paired away: at most n−m pairs each destroy one copy, leaving a surplus of at least 2m−n standing. The counter is just this brawl's bookkeeping, run left to right in two words of memory.
After
Pairwise cancellation. Every decrement pairs one copy of the candidate with one different element and discards both: and deleting two different values preserves any existing majority. A faction with m > n/2 members cannot be paired away: at most n−m pairs each destroy one copy, leaving a surplus of at least 2m−n standing. The counter is just this brawl's bookkeeping, run left to right in two words of memory.
Review and verification

Model: Pair cancellation preserves an existing majority; verification remains necessary.

060LZW / Growing phrase dictionaryTechnical correction

Every emission mints one new phrase, so the decoder can grow the identical dictionary one step behind: no codebook ever crosses the wire, and the one impossible code is forced, not guessed.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: Every emission mints one new phrase -> Every emission except the final one mints a new phrase
Text and code corrections (2)

src/content/lzw-growing-phrase-dictionary.jsx

Before
each
      emission registers one new phrase
After
each
      emission except the final one registers a new phrase

src/content/lzw-growing-phrase-dictionary.narration.js

Before
every emission also mints exactly one new phrase
After
every emission except the final one also mints exactly one new phrase
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/lzw-growing-phrase-dictionary.jsx

Before
The dictionary rides inside the data. Every emission mints one new phrase: the emitted phrase plus the byte that broke it: so the decoder, replaying the same rule one step behind, reconstructs the identical table from the codes alone. The one corner is the code used the instant it is minted: the decoder has not built it yet, but the timing forces its value: the last phrase plus that phrase's own first byte. Nothing is transmitted but the codes; the codebook is a shadow both sides compute.
After
The dictionary rides inside the data. Every emission except the final one mints a new phrase: the emitted phrase plus the byte that broke it: so the decoder, replaying the same rule one step behind, reconstructs the identical table from the codes alone. The one corner is the code used the instant it is minted: the decoder has not built it yet, but the timing forces its value: the last phrase plus that phrase's own first byte. Nothing is transmitted but the codes; the codebook is a shadow both sides compute.

After
ctx.fillText(`codes out: ${outBits} bits`, 18, 236);

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/gibbs-coordinate-conditionals.jsx

Before
The staircase and the ridge. Gibbs moves only along the axes: freeze y and redraw x from its conditional, then the reverse. On a round target the staircase strides anywhere in a couple of sweeps. On a ρ = 0.995 ridge every legal move is nearly perpendicular to the ridge direction, so the chain shuffles: acceptance 1.000, mixing time 199 sweeps, both measured. Acceptance is a property of the proposal; mixing is a property of the geometry.
After
The staircase and the ridge. Gibbs moves only along the axes: freeze y and redraw x from its conditional, then the reverse. On a round target the staircase strides anywhere in a couple of sweeps. On a ρ = 0.995 ridge every legal move is nearly perpendicular to the ridge direction, so the chain shuffles: acceptance 1.000, integrated autocorrelation time about 199 sweeps, both measured. Acceptance is a property of the proposal; mixing is a property of the geometry.

065Toom-Cook multiplication / Five-point interpolationTechnical correction

Three limbs make the product a quartic, and five points pin a quartic: evaluate, multiply post by post, interpolate back: five recursive products where pride pays nine.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: Multiplying polynomials coefficient-wise is nine cross products -> Multiplying two three-coefficient polynomials directly uses nine cross products
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/toom-cook-five-point-interpolation.jsx

Before
Five posts pin a quartic. Multiplying polynomials coefficient-wise is nine cross products; multiplying their values at a point is one number times one number. Evaluate both factors at 0, 1, −1, 2, ∞, multiply heights post by post, and the five product points determine the degree-4 product polynomial completely: interpolate back with a fixed 5×5 unwind whose divisions by 2 and 3 are exact (asserted). Five recursive multiplications instead of nine: the exponent falls from log₂3 = 1.585 to log₃5 = 1.465.
After
Five posts pin a quartic. Multiplying two three-coefficient polynomials directly uses nine cross products; multiplying their values at a point is one number times one number. Evaluate both factors at 0, 1, −1, 2, ∞, multiply heights post by post, and the five product points determine the degree-4 product polynomial completely: interpolate back with a fixed 5×5 unwind whose divisions by 2 and 3 are exact (asserted). Five recursive multiplications instead of nine: the exponent falls from log₂3 = 1.585 to log₃5 = 1.465.

After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/lis-patience-piles.jsx

Before
The deal is the proof. Each card lands on the leftmost pile whose top beats it, so piles only decrease: an increasing subsequence takes at most one card per pile. Each new pile was forced by a card bigger than every top: chase its backpointers leftward and a full climbing chain emerges, one card per pile. The count is squeezed to equality from both sides: and the same piles hand over Erdős-Szekeres (LIS × LDS ≥ n) and, dealt a random permutation, Ulam's 2√n: measured here at 93.7 against the ceiling of 100.
After
The deal is the proof. Each card lands on the leftmost pile whose top is at least as large, so piles never increase: an increasing subsequence takes at most one card per pile. Each new pile was forced by a card bigger than every top: chase its backpointers leftward and a full climbing chain emerges, one card per pile. The count is squeezed to equality from both sides: and the same piles hand over Erdős-Szekeres for distinct keys (LIS × LDS ≥ n) and, dealt a random permutation, Ulam's 2√n: measured here at 93.7 against the asymptotic estimate of 100.

075Burrows-Wheeler compression / Move-to-front plus RLETechnical correction

Sort every rotation, keep the last column: same letters, same entropy to the bit: but letters sorted into each other’s company by their shared futures, ready for a dumb coder to eat.

src/content/burrows-wheeler-mtf-rle.narration.js

Before
But now a coder with no memory whatsoever walks the shelf and meets the same letter, again and again: which is exactly the world a memoryless coder was built for.
After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/burrows-wheeler-mtf-rle.jsx

Before
Sorted by their futures. Every rotation of banana| lines up in sorted order, and the last column reads the letter BEFORE each context: the letter before every 'an…' is 'n', before every 'na…' is 'a': shared futures pull identical letters together. The column is a permutation: same letters, same entropy, to the bit: but the runs are new, and runs are what a memoryless coder can eat. The LF-mapping walks the column backward and rebuilds the text from nothing else.
After
Sorted by their futures. Every rotation of banana| lines up in sorted order, and the last column reads the letter BEFORE each context: the letters before 'an…' contexts are 'b' or 'n', while both 'na…' contexts have 'a' before them: shared futures pull identical letters together. The column is a permutation: same letters, same entropy, to the bit: but the runs are new, and move-to-front plus run-length coding turns them into a skewed stream for a memoryless coder. The LF-mapping walks the column backward and rebuilds the text from nothing else.

088Modular exponentiation / Square-and-multiplyTechnical correction

Read the exponent in binary: square for every bit, multiply on the 1s, reduce always: log-many operations where the naive ladder needs a 617-digit count: a building block of RSA and finite-field Diffie-Hellman.

Open lesson

  • Normalize the input base; correct naive and ladder operation counts; distinguish constant operation counts from constant-time cryptographic implementations.
Metadata corrections (2)

oneLiner

Before
Read the exponent in binary: square for every bit, multiply on the 1s, reduce always: log-many operations where the naive ladder needs a 617-digit count: the loop under every handshake.
After
Read the exponent in binary: square for every bit, multiply on the 1s, reduce always: log-many operations where the naive ladder needs a 617-digit count: a building block of RSA and finite-field Diffie-Hellman.

space

Before
O(1)
After
O(log e + log m) bits, including the binary exponent
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/modular-exponentiation-square-multiply.jsx

Before
The exponent read as binary. Each bit costs one squaring; each 1 costs one extra multiply; the modulus keeps every intermediate pocket-sized. The count law is exact: bit_length − 1 squares plus popcount − 1 multiplies: 3,091 operations at 2048 bits where the naive ladder's count is a 617-digit number. The same law is the leak: the count reads the exponent's 1s aloud (493-op gap measured between same-length keys), which is why secret exponents ride the Montgomery ladder's fixed rhythm instead.
After
The exponent read as binary. After the leading 1, each bit costs one squaring and each 1 costs one extra multiply. The count law is exact: bit_length − 1 squares plus popcount − 1 multiplies: 3,091 operations at 2048 bits where the naive ladder's count is a 617-digit number. The count reveals the exponent's popcount (493-op gap measured between same-length exponents). The ladder removes this count difference; this Python implementation is not constant-time or suitable for production secrets.

After
ctx.fillText(`${r0.arr.length} ops`, 150, r0.y + 32);
Review and verification

091Velocity Verlet dynamics / Symplectic time steppingTechnical correction

Kick, drift, kick: one force evaluation per step, and a symplectic structure that makes the energy error orbit in a band forever instead of marching: marathons need the shape, not the size.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: symplecticity means it follows the exact physics of a slightly-wrong Hamiltonian, so the energy error orbits in a band that never widens -> backward error analysis relates it to a nearby Hamiltonian, so at stable step sizes the energy error can stay bounded over long integrations

      is second order (measured 2.00)
After

src/content/velocity-verlet-symplectic.jsx

Before
by backward-error analysis it follows the exact trajectory of
      a slightly-wrong Hamiltonian rather than a slightly-wrong
      trajectory of the exact one. Energy cannot drift secularly,
      because a nearby conserved quantity is being conserved.
After
backward-error analysis relates it to a nearby modified
      Hamiltonian. At suitable stable step sizes this explains
      bounded energy errors over long integrations.

Before
a symplectic integrator follows the EXACT trajectory of a slightly wrong Hamiltonian, rather than a slightly wrong trajectory of the exact one
After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/velocity-verlet-symplectic.jsx

Before
Three integrators, one gravity. Euler's error compounds outward: the spiral of death, ×10⁴³ measured. RK4's fourth-order error is tiny and biased: it marches monotonically, draining the orbit: precision is not conservation. Verlet's second-order error is larger per step and structurally unbiased: symplecticity means it follows the exact physics of a slightly-wrong Hamiltonian, so the energy error orbits in a band that never widens: 3×10⁻⁵ across 200 periods, angular momentum at machine roundoff, and the whole run reversible to 10⁻¹². For marathons, the shape of the error matters more than its size.
After
Three integrators, one gravity. Euler's error compounds outward: the spiral of death, ×10⁴³ measured. RK4's fourth-order error is tiny and biased: it marches monotonically, draining the orbit: precision is not conservation. Verlet's second-order error is larger per step and structurally unbiased: backward error analysis relates it to a nearby Hamiltonian, so at stable step sizes the energy error can stay bounded over long integrations: 3×10⁻⁵ across 200 periods, angular momentum at machine roundoff, and the whole run reversible to 10⁻¹². For marathons, the shape of the error matters more than its size.

Photograph a running system without pausing it: each process records itself on first marker, and the markers sweep every channel clean: a consistent cut through causality, not time.

  • Figure caption: The composite state never existed at any wall-clock instant -> The composite state need not have existed at any wall-clock instant

      moment that ever existed on any wall clock: it is better: a

src/content/chandy-lamport-marker-snapshots.narration.js

Before
the composite is not a moment that ever existed on any wall clock
After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/chandy-lamport-marker-snapshots.jsx

Before
A cut through causality, not through time. Each process photographs itself on its first marker; the marker then sweeps each FIFO channel, and whatever arrives between the photo and the sweep IS the channel's in-flight content: recorded once, missed never. The composite state never existed at any wall-clock instant: it is a state the system could have occupied, which is exactly what conservation laws and stable properties need: measured here as 300 of 300 exact totals while the naive clock-style audit went 0 for 300 on the same storms.
After
A cut through causality, not through time. Each process photographs itself on its first marker; the marker then sweeps each FIFO channel, and whatever arrives between the photo and the sweep IS the channel's in-flight content: recorded once, missed never. The composite state need not have existed at any wall-clock instant: it is a state the system could have occupied, which is exactly what conservation laws and stable properties need: measured here as 300 of 300 exact totals while the naive clock-style audit went 0 for 300 on the same storms.
Layout corrections (1)

Before
measured: photos + channel records = 100,000 exactly, 300/300, ~7.2 transfers caught mid-channel per run
After
300/300: photos + channels = 100,000 · 7.2 transfers in transit per snapshot
Review and verification

Model: Chandy-Lamport FIFO markers, local states and channel recording.

103Gaussian mixture model / Expectation-maximizationTechnical correction

Model the data as overlapping tilted Gaussians and let every point belong fractionally to all of them: exact unregularized EM guarantees nondecreasing likelihood; the ridge-modified fit here is checked empirically.

Open lesson

  • Restrict the monotonic likelihood theorem to exact EM; permit legitimate likelihood decreases after ridge-modified updates.
Metadata corrections (1)

oneLiner

Before
Model the data as overlapping tilted Gaussians and let every point belong fractionally to all of them: guess softly, refit exactly, and a theorem guarantees the likelihood only climbs.
After
Model the data as overlapping tilted Gaussians and let every point belong fractionally to all of them: exact unregularized EM guarantees nondecreasing likelihood; the ridge-modified fit here is checked empirically.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/gaussian-mixture-expectation-maximization.jsx

Before
Guess softly, refit exactly, repeat. The E-step scores every point under every tilted ellipse and normalizes into responsibilities: the seam point belongs 0.6 to blue and 0.4 to amber, and 173 of 1,500 points here are genuinely shared like that. The M-step refits weights, means, and full covariances in closed form under those fractions. Each round provably raises the likelihood (asserted at all 119 steps): the staircase climbs to -5,526.2, above the generating parameters' own -5,536.7, recovering every planted mean within 0.13.
After
Guess softly, refit, repeat. The E-step scores every point under every tilted ellipse and normalizes into responsibilities: the seam point belongs 0.6 to blue and 0.4 to amber, and 173 of 1,500 points here have top responsibility below 0.9. The M-step refits weights, means, and full covariances, then adds a diagonal ridge. Likelihood increased on these 119 steps, checked numerically; the ridge update does not guarantee monotonicity in general. The fit ends at -5,526.2, above the generating parameters' own -5,536.7, recovering every planted mean within 0.13.

After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/treap-random-priorities.jsx

Before
Two orders, one tree, and the dice hold the shape. Keys read left-to-right in BST order; priorities decrease downward in heap order; the unique tree satisfying both is a canonical function of the (key, priority) set, independent of arrival order (proven here across three orders, node for node). Random draws make that shape behave like a random-order BST: average depth 20.4 at n = 100,000 against the 2 ln n = 23.0 expectation: while sequential arrival, which stretches a plain BST to depth 4,000 (2,007 visits per lookup), leaves the treap at 14.8.
After

Random draws make that shape behave like a random-order BST: average depth 20.4 at n = 100,000 against the leading asymptotic term 2 ln n = 23.0: while sequential arrival, which stretches a plain BST to depth 4,000 (2,007 visits per lookup), leaves the treap at 14.8.

src/content/lsd-radix-digit-passes.jsx

116Paxos / Proposer-acceptor quorumsTechnical correction

Two phases addressed to everyone, and nothing counts until a majority answers: any two majorities share an acceptor, that acceptor remembers, and once a value is chosen every later ballot is forced to choose it again.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: the pairs stop overlapping -> the pairs can be disjoint
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/paxos-proposer-acceptor-quorums.jsx

Before
Any two majorities of five share at least one acceptor, and that acceptor remembers. Proposer P1 wins promises from {a0, a1, a2} and lands accept(1, A) on a majority: A is chosen. P2 arrives with ballot 2 and collects promises from {a2, a3, a4}; the overlap a2 reports (1, A), so P2 is bound to propose A, and the second majority re-chooses it. Checked exhaustively: 1,439,849 states, one value ever chosen. Shrink the quorum to two of five and the pairs stop overlapping: two values in 453 states.
After
Any two majorities of five share at least one acceptor, and that acceptor remembers. Proposer P1 wins promises from {a0, a1, a2} and lands accept(1, A) on a majority: A is chosen. P2 arrives with ballot 2 and collects promises from {a2, a3, a4}; the overlap a2 reports (1, A), so P2 is bound to propose A, and the second majority re-chooses it. Checked exhaustively: 1,439,849 states, one value ever chosen. Shrink the quorum to two of five and the pairs can be disjoint: two values in 453 states.

118Knuth's Algorithm X / Dancing linksTechnical correction

Pick the column with the fewest rows, try each row, cover and recurse, then let the links dance back: undo for the price of do, and one engine for queens, sudoku, and every exactly-once puzzle.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: Covering a column unlinks its header and every colliding row this way -> Covering a column unlinks its header horizontally and the colliding rows from other columns vertically
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/knuths-algorithm-x-dancing-links.jsx

Before
A node leaves its row by two pointer writes and returns by two more. Removed: x.left.right = x.right and x.right.left = x.left; the node itself still holds both neighbors. Restored, in reverse order of removal: x.left.right = x and x.right.left = x. Covering a column unlinks its header and every colliding row this way; uncovering replays it backwards. Measured: 8-queens 1,198 nodes with the smallest-column rule against 2,056 without; the sudoku 2,740 against unfinished at 200,000; copying instead of dancing costs 31.8× the undo work at 561 rows.
After
A node leaves its row by two pointer writes and returns by two more. Removed: x.left.right = x.right and x.right.left = x.left; the node itself still holds both neighbors. Restored, in reverse order of removal: x.left.right = x and x.right.left = x. Covering a column unlinks its header horizontally and the colliding rows from other columns vertically; uncovering replays it backwards. Measured: 8-queens 1,198 nodes with the smallest-column rule against 2,056 without; the sudoku 2,740 against unfinished at 200,000; copying instead of dancing costs 31.8× the undo work at 561 rows.

119Hopcroft's minimization / Partition refinementTechnical correction

Split blocks of states through their inverse transitions from a queue of splitters, and after every split queue only the smaller half: a state re-enters the queue at most log n times, and the quadratic rounds become n log n.

Open lesson

  • Retain both splitter pieces when replacing an already queued block; correct 4,800 choose 2 to 11,517,600 table entries; scope the table-filling and Brzozowski comparison checks to the cases actually tested.
  • Figure caption: Of the two pieces, only the smaller (amber) is queued -> If the old block is already queued, both pieces replace it; otherwise only the smaller (amber) is queued
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

and after every split queues only the smaller piece.
After
and after a split queues only the smaller piece unless the old block was already queued, when both pieces replace it.

src/content/hopcrofts-minimization-partition-refinement.jsx

Before
of the two halves produced by a split, only the smaller half
      goes back into the queue. Anyone who lands in the queue again
      has been halved at least once
After

After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/hopcrofts-minimization-partition-refinement.jsx

Before
A splitter is a block and a letter. The states whose a-transitions land in the splitter (blue ring) are collected through the inverse edges, and every block they straddle splits into the part inside and the part outside. Of the two pieces, only the smaller (amber) is queued, so a state re-enters the queue at most log₂ n times per letter: O(k n log n) total. Measured: 20,105 examinations on 4,800 states under a bound of 352,190; 189,898 when both halves are queued; Moore 57,600 in 4 rounds on the same machine and 2,095,104 in 1,023 rounds on the 1,024-state chain, where Hopcroft examines 2,048.
After
A splitter is a block and a letter. The states whose a-transitions land in the splitter (blue ring) are collected through the inverse edges, and every block they straddle splits into the part inside and the part outside. If the old block is already queued, both pieces replace it; otherwise only the smaller (amber) is queued, so a state re-enters the queue at most log₂ n times per letter: O(k n log n) total. Measured: 20,105 examinations on 4,800 states under a bound of 352,190; 189,898 when both halves are queued; Moore 57,600 in 4 rounds on the same machine and 2,095,104 in 1,023 rounds on the 1,024-state chain, where Hopcroft examines 2,048.

128AdaBoost / Exponential reweightingVisual clarity improvement

129DBSCAN / Density-reachabilityTechnical correction

A point with enough neighbors within epsilon is core; a cluster is whatever chains of core points can reach, and a stray point with no chain to join is noise. Crescents come out whole because membership follows density, never distance to a center.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: a stray point with fewer than 5 neighbors has no chain to join -> a stray point outside every core point’s ε-neighborhood has no chain to join
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/dbscan-density-reachability.jsx

Before
The scene and the rule. Two crescents (blue and green), a blob (purple), and stray noise (red crosses). The amber circle is one density test: ε = 0.2 around a point, minPts = 5 inside it makes the point core. Chains of core points at most ε apart carry membership along each crescent and never across the gap, which is wider than ε; a stray point with fewer than 5 neighbors has no chain to join. Measured: 3 clusters, 73 noise, 521 cores, 6 borders, ARI 0.901; k-means 0.372.
After
The scene and the rule. Two crescents (blue and green), a blob (purple), and stray noise (red crosses). The amber circle is one density test: ε = 0.2 around a point, minPts = 5 inside it makes the point core. Chains of core points at most ε apart carry membership along each crescent and never across the gap, which is wider than ε; a stray point outside every core point’s ε-neighborhood has no chain to join. Measured: 3 clusters, 73 noise, 521 cores, 6 borders, ARI 0.901; k-means 0.372.
Review and verification

Model: DBSCAN core expansion versus border/noise distinction.

130Perceptron / Mistake-driven updatesTechnical correction

Sweep the points; on a mistake add the signed point to the weights, on a hit do nothing. The line is a sum of its mistakes, and Novikoff's theorem says how many there can be: sixty runs, sixty counts under the bound.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: a sum of the points it got wrong -> a sum of the signed points it got wrong
  • Clip the separating line to the plotted disk.

Before
Two classes in the unit disk with a gap, the widest-margin direction u found by an independent sweep (γ = the smallest y (u · x)), and the perceptron’s final line, which is a sum of the points it got wrong (amber rings). Novikoff: mistakes ≤ (R/γ)². Measured at gaps 0.4 / 0.2 / 0.1: γ 0.203 / 0.106 / 0.054, bounds 49 / 179 / 698, worst mistakes 10 / 16 / 20 across 20 shuffles each; updating on every example never converges.

131First fit decreasing / Descending size orderTechnical correction

Sort the items largest first, then drop each into the first bin with room. The big items claim bins before anything blocks them and the small ones fill the gaps: optimal on two hundred of two hundred small instances, and never worse than eleven ninths.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: Johnson’s [restricted source word], the tight worst case. -> Johnson’s construction, the asymptotic worst case.
  • Figure caption: 11/9 exactly, and Dósa proved nothing worse can happen. -> 11/9 on this construction; Dósa’s tight general bound is FFD ≤ (11/9) OPT + 6/9.
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

Before
Johnson’s [restricted source word], the tight worst case. Thirty items: six of 1/2+ε, six of 1/4+2ε, six of 1/4+ε, twelve of 1/4−2ε (ε = 0.01). Left: the optimal packing, 9 bins, each verified to hold at most 1: six bins of {1/2+ε, 1/4+ε, 1/4−2ε} and three of two 1/4+2ε with two 1/4−2ε. Right: first fit decreasing opens 11: the six largest each take one 1/4+2ε and then nothing else fits beside them, the 1/4+ε items go three to a bin, and the twelve smallest four to a bin. 11/9 exactly, and Dósa proved nothing worse can happen.
After

142Betweenness centrality / Brandes accumulationTechnical correction

One breadth-first search per source counts the shortest paths to every vertex; one pass back hands each vertex's dependency to its predecessors in proportion to those counts. Every path counted, none listed, and the bridge stands out where degree cannot see it.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: which adds up along the tree -> which adds up along the shortest-path DAG
  • Figure caption: walks the same tree backward -> walks the same DAG backward
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/betweenness-centrality-brandes-accumulation.jsx

Before
One source, two passes. Left: the breadth-first search from s labels each vertex with its distance and σ, the number of shortest paths from s, which adds up along the tree (σ of a vertex is the sum of σ over its predecessors). Right: the accumulation walks the same tree backward, each vertex passing (σ(v)/σ(w))(1 + δ(w)) to each predecessor v, so δ at a vertex is its share of every shortest path from s that passes through it. Summed over sources: betweenness. Measured: 15.1× fewer operations than the definition at n = 240, and 48,620 grid paths counted from one search.
After
One source, two passes. Left: the breadth-first search from s labels each vertex with its distance and σ, the number of shortest paths from s, which adds up along the shortest-path DAG (σ of a vertex is the sum of σ over its predecessors). Right: the accumulation walks the same DAG backward, each vertex passing (σ(v)/σ(w))(1 + δ(w)) to each predecessor v, so δ at a vertex is its share of every shortest path from s that passes through it. Summed over sources: betweenness. Measured: 15.1× fewer operations than the definition at n = 240, and 48,620 grid paths counted from one search.

143Gossip protocol / Random-peer anti-entropyVisual clarity improvement

Every round, every node reconciles with one uniformly random peer: the spread doubles while few know and finishes the stragglers by luck, in about log N rounds, with no coordinator and through crashes. Fix the peer and it takes N rounds; drop the reconciliation and some nodes never hear.

145RANSAC / Random consensus samplingTechnical correction

Fit the model to the smallest sample that determines it, let every point vote on it within a threshold, keep the largest consensus over enough random draws that one is surely clean, and refit to the voters alone: the outliers never enter the answer.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: A draw that includes an outlier (dashed amber) fits almost nobody and is discarded. -> The illustrated draw that includes an outlier (dashed amber) fits almost nobody and is discarded.
  • Figure caption: the outliers never enter it -> only points within its threshold enter it, which can include some outliers

Least squares on all 200 points (red dashed) is pulled toward the scatter: 0.278 mean slope error at 40% outliers against RANSAC’s worst of 0.023 over 200 trials.
After

The illustrated draw that includes an outlier (dashed amber) fits almost nobody and is discarded. After N draws the largest consensus is refit by least squares; only points within its threshold enter it, which can include some outliers. Least squares on all 200 points (red dashed) is pulled toward the scatter: 0.278 mean slope error at 40% outliers against RANSAC’s worst of 0.023 over 200 trials.

147Chinese remainder theorem / Garner's algorithmTechnical correction

Rebuild an integer from its residues as mixed-radix digits: small modular updates, products up to twice the largest modulus bit width, then full-width assembly.

Metadata corrections (3)

oneLiner

Before
Rebuild an integer from its residues as mixed-radix digits, one small inverse per modulus, so nothing exceeds one modulus until the final assembly: the theorem's guarantee at a word's width instead of the big product's.
After
Rebuild an integer from its residues as mixed-radix digits: small modular updates, products up to twice the largest modulus bit width, then full-width assembly.

time

Before
k(k - 1)/2 small multiplies, one assembly
After

space

Before

After

Caption corrections (1)

src/content/chinese-remainder-theorem-garners-algorithm.jsx

Before
The integer 551 seen through moduli 7, 11, 13 (M = 1,001): residues 5, 1, 5. Garner’s ladder finds the mixed-radix digits 5, 1, 7 with one small inverse per rung and assembles 5 + 7 · 1 + 77 · 7 = 551; nothing exceeds 13 until the last line. The direct formula computes the same 551 as 5 · 143 · 5 + 1 · 91 · 4 + 5 · 77 · 12 = 8,559 mod 1,001, every term larger than M. On ten 64-bit moduli the gap is 64 bits against 704.
After
The integer 551 seen through moduli 7, 11, 13 (M = 1,001): residues 5, 1, 5. Garner’s ladder finds digits 5, 1, 7 using three pairwise inverses and assembles 5 + 7 · 1 + 77 · 7 = 551. Reduced digits stay below their moduli; multiplication results can exceed them. The direct formula computes the same 551 as 5 · 143 · 5 + 1 · 91 · 4 + 5 · 77 · 12 = 8,559 mod 1,001. On ten 64-bit moduli, Garner’s update products reach 128 bits and its assembly 640; direct terms reach 704 bits and their sum 706.
Layout corrections (1)

src/content/chinese-remainder-theorem-garners-algorithm.jsx

Before
v3 = ((5 − 5) · 7⁻¹ − 1) · 11⁻¹ mod 13 = 12 · 6 mod 13 = 7
After
v3 = (0 − 1) · 6 mod 13 = 7

158TCP congestion control / AIMD slow startTechnical correction

Double the window until the first loss, then add one per round trip and halve on every loss: from one bit of feedback, competing senders converge to equal shares, because increases move parallel to the fairness line and halvings move toward the origin.

Open lesson

159Barnes-Hut / Octree center-of-mass approximationTechnical correction

Put the bodies in an octree with every cell's mass and center of mass, and let a far cell pull as one point when its size over its distance is below theta: a fifth of the pair interactions for a fraction of a percent of force error, with the trade measured at five angles.

160Levenberg-Marquardt / Trust-region dampingTechnical correction

Solve the Gauss-Newton normal equations with lambda added to the diagonal, raising lambda tenfold after a rejected step and lowering it after an accepted one: a gradient step when the linear model lies, the full Newton step when it tells the truth, and the superlinear finish of Newton from starts where Newton alone diverges.

Open lesson

  • Stop converged/stalled LM; cap damping; name reference cost honestly.
  • Figure caption: to the floor of 0.0203 -> to the fitted cost of 0.0203
  • Figure caption: the last steps are pure Gauss-Newton -> the last steps approach Gauss-Newton
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

Animation correction: Stop converged/stalled LM; cap damping; name reference cost honestly.

161Nelder-Mead / Reflect-expand-contract simplexTechnical correction

Keep n + 1 points sorted by value and replace the worst each iteration by reflecting it through the centroid of the others, expanding when the reflection is the new best, contracting when it is poor, and shrinking everything toward the best when nothing helps: a minimizer built from comparisons alone, which is why it survives noise that destroys finite-difference gradients.

Open lesson

  • Determine outside-contraction label before replacing the worst vertex.
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

Animation correction: Determine outside-contraction label before replacing the worst vertex.

162Register allocation / Graph coloringVisual clarity improvement

163Fibonacci heap / Lazy consolidation with markingTechnical correction

Keep a forest of heap-ordered trees in a lazy root list: insert splices a one-node tree, decrease-key cuts a node loose, and only delete-min tidies by linking equal-degree roots; a node that loses a second child is cut too, which keeps every tree fat enough that the maximum degree stays logarithmic and decrease-key costs a constant, amortized.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: cuts per decrease-key against the bound of 2, and links per delete-min against log₂ n -> mean cuts per decrease-key against an amortized reference of 2, and mean links per delete-min against log₂ n for scale

165AVL tree / Height-balance rotationsTechnical correction

Store a height in every node, allow the two children of any node to differ in height by at most one, and repair a broken node on the way back up with a single or double rotation at the lowest place it breaks: the height stays under 1.44 log2 n for any arrival order, an insertion needs at most one rotation, and the sorted input that turns a plain tree into a list builds the perfectly balanced floor instead.

Open lesson

  • Fit the tree depth within its panel.

166Counterfactual regret minimization / Regret-matching updatesTechnical correction

Walk the whole game tree in self-play, and at every information set add, for each action, the gain it would have had over the strategy played, weighted by how likely the opponent and chance were to bring the game there; next iteration play each action in proportion to its positive regret: the average of everything played converges to a Nash equilibrium at the rate of one over the square root of the iterations, with no learning rate and no linear program.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: on Kuhn's [restricted source word] constraint -> on Kuhn's equilibrium constraint
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/counterfactual-regret-minimization-regret-matching-updates.jsx

Before
Left: exploitability of the average strategy on Kuhn poker against iterations, both axes logarithmic, for CFR, CFR+, fictitious play, and outcome-sampling MCCFR, with the 1/√T slope for reference. Right: the first player's bet probability by card at the first decision, on Kuhn's [restricted source word] constraint P(bet | K) = 3 P(bet | J).
After
Left: exploitability of the average strategy on Kuhn poker against iterations, both axes logarithmic, for CFR, CFR+, fictitious play, and outcome-sampling MCCFR, with the 1/√T slope for reference. Right: the first player's bet probability by card at the first decision, on Kuhn's equilibrium constraint P(bet | K) = 3 P(bet | J).

167Brent's method / Inverse quadratic interpolationVisual clarity improvement

Keep a bracket that provably holds the root, propose the next point from the sideways parabola through the last three (the secant when two coincide), and take it only if it stays inside the bracket and is less than half the step before last, falling back to the midpoint otherwise: superlinear on smooth functions, never worse than about the square of bisection's count on any continuous one.

168Greedy graph coloring / DSatur saturation orderingTechnical correction

Give each vertex the smallest color its colored neighbors have not used, taking the vertices in the order that is hardest to fool: the one whose neighbors already show the most distinct colors goes next, ties broken by the most uncolored neighbors; one pass, at most one more color than the largest degree, and an optimal coloring on every bipartite graph.

169BM25 ranking / Saturating term frequencyVisual clarity improvement

Walk each query term's postings and add idf times a term frequency that saturates, tf (k1 + 1) / (tf + k1 (1 - b + b |d| / avgdl)), so the fifth occurrence of a word is worth far less than the first, two different query words beat one word twice, and a document ten times longer is not ten times more relevant.

170Black-Scholes / Closed-form European pricingTechnical correction

Hedge the option with the stock and the stock's expected return drops out, so the price is the discounted expected payoff under a measure where the stock drifts at the risk-free rate; assume constant volatility and the terminal price is lognormal, so that expectation is two normal distribution functions, C = S N(d1) - K e^(-rT) N(d2), and every sensitivity is a formula too.

Open lesson

173Canny edge detector / Hysteresis thresholdingVisual clarity improvement

Blur, take the gradient, keep only the local maxima along the gradient so every edge is one pixel thin, then decide with two thresholds instead of one: a pixel above the high threshold is an edge, and a pixel above the low threshold is an edge only if it connects through other candidates to a strong one, so faint stretches of real contours survive and isolated noise does not.

175Nim solving / Nimber XORTechnical correction

Write each heap in binary and XOR them: the player to move is lost exactly when the result is zero, and from any nonzero result one heap can be reduced to make it zero again, so perfect play costs one XOR instead of a game tree; by the Sprague-Grundy theorem the same number values every impartial game and adds across independent components.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: Incorrect xor and winning move -> 7 xor10 xor12 =1; reduce7 to6.
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/nim-solving-nimber-xor.jsx

Before
Left: heaps of 7, 10, and 12 in binary. The column for 2 has three ones, the column for 1 has one, so the nim-sum is 0110 = 6 and the position is a win for the player to move; the heap with a one in the highest unbalanced column (the 4s column: heaps 7 and 12 both have it, either works) is reduced by XOR with 6: 12 becomes 10, every column balances, and the opponent faces a nim-sum of zero. Right: nodes visited by plain minimax on three equal heaps (red) against the memoized position count (gray) and the three XOR operations (blue), log scale.
After
Left: heaps of 7, 10, and 12 in binary. The 8s, 4s, and 2s columns each have two ones; only the 1s column is unbalanced, so the nim-sum is 0001 = 1 and the position is a win for the player to move. The heap of 7 is reduced by XOR with 1 to 6: every column balances, and the opponent faces a nim-sum of zero. Right: nodes visited by plain minimax on three equal heaps (red) against the memoized position count (gray) and the three XOR operations (blue), log scale.
Review and verification

Model: Nim xor move and corrected 7,10,12 example.

Checks: Actual source preserves zero/nonzero nim-sum alternation and final XOR-player victory; 20-cycle previous invariant coverage

176M/M/1 analysis / Birth-death balance equationsVisual clarity improvement

Model the number in the system as a chain that climbs at the arrival rate and falls at the service rate, cut it between every two neighboring states and equate the flows, and the whole distribution is geometric, each extra customer rho times as likely as the last: L = rho / (1 - rho), W = 1 / (mu - lambda), P(wait) = rho, with Little's law for free and the same recursion for any number of servers.

177Differential privacy / Laplace mechanismTechnical correction

Add Laplace noise with scale equal to the most one record can change the answer, divided by epsilon: the output densities for any two databases differing in one record then stay within a factor of e to the epsilon everywhere, which is the definition of differential privacy, and the price is a mean absolute error of exactly that scale, with budgets that add across releases.

Open lesson

  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

178Hill climbing / Random restartsVisual clarity improvement

Move to the best neighbor until nothing improves, and when that local optimum is not the goal, throw the state away and climb again from a fresh random start: if one climb succeeds with probability p, the climbs until success are geometric with mean 1/p, so a method that is complete almost never becomes complete almost surely, with no parameter but the budget.

179Kernel density estimation / Silverman bandwidthVisual clarity improvement

Center a Gaussian bump of width h on every observation and average them, and set h by the one-line rule 0.9 min(s, IQR/1.34) n^(-1/5), the bandwidth that is optimal for Gaussian data with a robust scale: nearly the oracle on one cloud of points, and a blur on two, because the scale it measures is then the gap between the modes.

180Gray code generation / Reflected binaryTechnical correction

List every n-bit string so that neighbors differ in exactly one bit by taking the shorter list with a leading zero and then its mirror image with a leading one: the seam is one flip like every other step, the recursion collapses to g(i) = i XOR (i >> 1) with the ruler sequence naming the flipped bit, and a walk through all subsets changes one element at a time.

181Tower of Hanoi / Recursive halvingTechnical correction

To move n disks, move the top n - 1 to the spare peg, move the largest disk once, and move the n - 1 back onto it: the plan is the instruction applied to itself, the count is 2^n - 1 and provably the fewest, and the same sequence falls out of an iterative rule, of binary counting, and of the Gray code.

182Monte Carlo integration / Uniform samplingTechnical correction

Write the integral as the box volume times the mean of the function, estimate that mean by evaluating the function at points drawn independently and uniformly over the box, and read the error off the sample standard deviation over the square root of the count: unbiased, self-reporting, and shrinking as one over root N in any number of dimensions, where a grid of m points per axis costs m to the power d.

183Round-robin scheduling / Time quantumTechnical correction

Keep a FIFO ready queue and let the head run for one quantum before it goes to the back: every job gets a turn within one lap of the queue, so a short job never waits out a long one, and the quantum's length is the dial between responsiveness and the cost of switching, with no knowledge of any burst required.

Open lesson

  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

184Bonferroni correction / [restricted source word]-wise error controlTechnical correction

Run m tests at once and reject each only at alpha over m: the union bound then caps the chance of any false claim across the whole [restricted source word] at alpha, whatever the tests' dependence, and the price is that every real effect must clear alpha over m instead of alpha.

185Matrix exponentiation / Binary exponentiationVisual clarity improvement

Turn one step of a linear recurrence into a companion matrix, so n steps are the n-th power, and take that power by squaring once per bit of n and multiplying in at the one bits: floor(log2 n) + popcount(n) - 1 products instead of n - 1, for Fibonacci at a quintillion, for walk counts on a graph, for anything associative.

186Ski rental / Break-even deterministic ruleVisual clarity improvement

Rent for 1 a day or buy for B with the season length unknown: rent until the rent paid would equal the price, then buy on day B. Whatever day the season ends you pay at most 2 − 1/B times the clairvoyant, and no deterministic rule does better; a coin gets e/(e − 1), a trusted forecast gets more and risks everything.

187Reaching definitions / Forward dataflowVisual clarity improvement

Give every block a gen set (its last write of each variable) and a kill set (every other definition of those variables), start all IN and OUT empty, and pop blocks off a worklist seeded in reverse postorder: IN is the union of the predecessors' OUT, OUT is gen plus whatever in IN survives kill, and a successor is re-queued only when OUT changed; because union distributes over these transfer functions, the fixpoint is exactly the meet over all paths, reached in about two visits per block.

1882-opt / Edge-uncrossing swapsVisual clarity improvement

Take any tour, find two edges whose swap (reconnect a to c and b to d, reversing the path between them) is shorter, apply it, and repeat until a full pass finds none: in the plane every crossing is such a swap, so the local optimum is crossing-free, each test costs four distance lookups, and sorted neighbor lists make a pass near-linear.

189Vickrey auction / Second-price ruleTechnical correction

Seal the bids, give the item to the highest, and charge the second bid: your bid then decides only whether you win, never what you pay, so the truth is a dominant strategy, the highest value always wins, and the seller earns exactly what a first-price auction would at equilibrium, without anyone computing one.

191Perlin noise / Gradient lattice interpolationTechnical correction

Hash every integer lattice point to a unit gradient, make the surface zero there with that slope, and blend the four corner planes with the quintic fade 6t^5 - 15t^4 + 10t^3 so slope and curvature join across cells: a smooth, bounded, stateless texture at any point and scale, with octaves at halving amplitude for terrain.

192Random search / Uniform hyperparameter samplingVisual clarity improvement

Draw every trial's hyperparameters independently and uniformly (log-uniform across orders of magnitude), train, keep the best: n trials hit the top 5% of the space with probability 1 - 0.95^n in any dimension, and because only a few knobs matter, n random trials try n values of the one that does where a grid of the same size tries k.

193Distance-vector routing / Bellman-Ford exchangeVisual clarity improvement

Every router keeps one vector, its best known distance and next hop to each destination, hands it to its neighbors every update interval, and relaxes to the cheapest offer plus the link: Bellman-Ford spread across the routers, settling in L minus one intervals, and counting a dead route up to a finite infinity when a link dies.

195Shannon entropy estimation / Maximum likelihood plug-inTechnical correction

Count each symbol, divide by n, and plug the frequencies into Shannon's formula: one pass, nothing to tune, and a bias that is always negative and known to first order, (k − 1)/(2n ln 2) bits, so that at ten samples per symbol one added term makes it honest and at one sample per symbol nothing does.

Open lesson

197Gaussian process regression / RBF kernelTechnical correction

Put a Gaussian prior over whole functions, condition it on the data with one Cholesky factorization, and read off a mean and a variance at every input: the squared-exponential kernel supplies the smoothness and one length scale, the marginal likelihood from the same factor chooses the scale, and the error bar is checked against functions drawn from the prior.

198Fruchterman-Reingold / Force-directed placementTechnical correction

Treat a graph as charges and springs: every pair repels by k squared over d, every edge pulls by d squared over k with k the ideal edge length, each vertex moves along its net force by at most a temperature that cools linearly to zero, and the resting positions are the drawing: adjacent near, everything else apart, symmetry recovered because the forces carry it.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: the edge length the system settles toward -> the equilibrium distance for an isolated pair; other vertices change it
  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.

After

Right: scale-free stress of five layouts of the 6 × 6 grid from the same random start, with crossing counts; 0 is a perfect embedding of graph distance.

199Sharding / Hash partitioningTechnical correction

Give every key one home by hashing it and taking the remainder modulo the shard count: loads as even as a multinomial, no directory, one machine per lookup, and a bill that comes due only when N changes, when the modulo re-homes N/(N + 1) of the data where a ring or jump hash moves 1/(N + 1), and when one hot key needs a salt because no hash can split it.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: a consistent-hashing ring or jump consistent hash (blue, 1/(N + 1) -> a consistent-hashing ring or jump consistent hash (blue, expected 1/(N + 1)

200Template matching / Normalized cross-correlationTechnical correction

Slide the template over every window and score each by the correlation coefficient of the two pixel sets, mean removed and variance normalized, so any gain or offset in the lighting still scores exactly one at the true match; read each window's mean and variance from two summation tables in four lookups, floor the variance to refuse flat windows, and fit a quadratic around the peak for the sub-pixel position.

Left: of 24 scenes whose brightness was rescaled by a random gain and offset, how many times each score put its best window on the planted pattern. Normalized cross-correlation is exactly invariant to the change; the sum of squared differences and the sum of absolute differences are not; raw correlation mostly survives because the pattern’s own dot product still dominates. Right: multiplications per full match on a log scale. The summation tables cut the normalization from 404,496 to 4,096; the FFT numerator costs more than the direct dot products at this scene size.
After

Normalized cross-correlation is exactly invariant to a positive gain and an offset; the sum of squared differences and the sum of absolute differences are not; raw correlation mostly survives because the pattern’s own dot product still dominates. Right: multiplications per full match on a log scale. The summation tables cut the normalization from 404,496 to 4,096; the FFT numerator costs more than the direct dot products at this scene size.

201Importance sampling / Variance-reducing proposalTechnical correction

Draw from a proposal that lands on the rare event, weight every sample by the target-to-proposal density ratio, and the weighted average stays unbiased while its variance drops by thousands: the exponential tilt N(4, 1) estimates P(Z > 4) to 1.75% in 10,000 draws where 100,000 plain draws manage 49%, and Kish's effective sample size tells you when the proposal has failed.

Open lesson

  • Separate panel legends.

After

202Longest prefix match / Trie lookupVisual clarity improvement

Route every packet by the most specific prefix that covers its destination: store the prefixes in a binary trie keyed by address bits, walk the destination one bit per level remembering the last prefix passed, and answer in at most 32 steps where a scan compares the whole table, with strides for speed and path compression for memory as the measured rivals.

203LL(1) parsing / First-follow tableVisual clarity improvement

Keep a stack of the symbols still to match and look at one token: compute nullable, FIRST, and FOLLOW as least fixpoints, enter each production under the tokens that can begin it (and, when it can vanish, under the tokens that can follow it), and parse by one table lookup per expansion; a cell with two entries is the proof that the grammar is not LL(1) yet.

204Greedy vertex cover / Maximal matchingTechnical correction

Take any edge with neither end chosen, put both ends in the cover, delete what they touch, repeat: the kept edges are a maximal matching, every cover needs one vertex per kept edge, so the output is at most twice the optimum in one linear pass, with the matching as a receipt; the max-degree greedy beats it on average and loses by a logarithm when the graph is chosen against it.

Open lesson

  • Figure caption: the bad [restricted source word]. -> the bad construction.
  • Figure caption: because the theorem forbids anything worse -> attaining the theorem’s upper bound on this construction
  • Keep statistics clear of the graph.
Caption corrections (1)

src/content/greedy-vertex-cover-maximal-matching.jsx

Before
Left: ratio of cover size to the exact optimum over 300 random graphs of 14 vertices, mean and worst, for the matching rule, the max-degree greedy, and LP rounding. The greedy wins on average; the two provable methods touch exactly 2 at worst and never pass it. Right: the bad [restricted source word]. The degree greedy’s ratio climbs with the harmonic number as n doubles from 16 to 128, while the matching rule sits at 2.000 on every graph, because the theorem forbids anything worse.
After
Left: ratio of cover size to the exact optimum over 300 random graphs of 14 vertices, mean and worst, for the matching rule, the max-degree greedy, and LP rounding. The degree greedy’s ratio climbs with the harmonic number as n doubles from 16 to 128, while the matching rule sits at 2.000 on every graph, attaining the theorem’s upper bound on this construction.

205Hidden Markov map matching / Emission-transition road probabilitiesVisual clarity improvement

Treat roads as hidden states and GPS fixes as noisy observations, score each fix by its distance to a road and each hop by how far the driving distance strays from the straight line, and let Viterbi pick the most probable route: 95.6% of fixes on the true segment with half a percent of route-length error where nearest-road snapping manages 90.6% and a 47% detour.

206Lexicographic permutations / Next-permutation algorithmTechnical correction

Scan from the right for the longest non-increasing suffix, swap the element before it with the rightmost suffix element larger than it, reverse the suffix: Narayana's fourteenth-century rule visits every arrangement of a multiset exactly once in dictionary order, in place with two indices, at a constant amortized cost per step (mean suffix e - 1), where the single-swap orders of Heap and Johnson-Trotter are cheaper per step and unsorted.

Open lesson

  • Shorten or wrap canvas labels and adjust spacing to fit the panel; checked at ten sampled states.
Metadata corrections (1)

time

Before
O(1) amortized per arrangement (mean suffix e − 1); O(n) for one worst step
After
O(1) amortized for distinct keys; O(n) worst step
